ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U8	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The Riigikogu Committee on Monetary Affairs discussed this draft on Tuesday, 16 November. For example, Commissioner Aivar Sõerd asked whether the decision to reform the ESM was taken at the level of finance ministers during the previous coalition and, furthermore, if the ESM has made a profit over the years, whether any changes in the distribution of profits have taken place. On top of this, Minister of Finance Keit Pentus-Rosimannus replied that this has been a cross-coalition issue that has started quite a few good years ago. In particular, this change started in 2018; in November 2020 the Government of the Republic has confirmed the positions of the Government in advance of the Council of Finance Ministers for the last time, and no changes in the distribution of profits are foreseen. Member of the Commission Jürgen Ligi asked about preventive support instruments, on which the Minister for Finance replied that Member States would have the opportunity to receive support from the ESM before any major problems and serious difficulties arise in lending money. The reform clarifies the process of monitoring compliance with the eligibility criteria. Andres Kuning, Head of the European Union and International Cooperation Department of the Ministry of Finance, added that the capacity and capacity to service debt is a universal principle that actually applies to all ESM instruments. If anyone wants support, the ESM must provide this security to the shareholders on condition that this loan also returns. In conclusion, it was decided to propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda by 24 November (consensus), that the first reading be closed (also a consensus), that Mr Dmitri Dmitriev (consensus) be appointed representative of the lead committee and that the deadline for the amendments be set at 5.15 p.m. on 2 December (which was also decided by consensus). Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U12	The ESM, the same European stability mechanism, was established in 2012. Our party was not in Parliament at the time, but we were already vehemently opposed to it at that time. Estonia, Estonian taxpayers, Estonian people were placed indirectly and directly responsible for the debts of others in order to save our euro. It was decided to expand the use of the money collected by ESM in 2018 at that time the Estonian Conservative People's Party was not in the government, and the entire decision was made at government level. This change will make it easier to use the ESM money and increase the possibility that it will be used. Simply put, the possibility of us losing our money is growing considerably. When I was in government, I opposed this same reform of the ESM at my first meeting of finance ministers. I opposed extending the use of money and simplifying its use. Unfortunately, the next day, Prime Minister Jüri Ratas announced that he would cancel my blockade and that the reform would go on. At this point, I underline that the Prime Minister, who is the highest negotiator in the European Union according to Estonian rules, made his position pay despite my opposition. Later, I refused to attend the European Union finance ministers' meetings, where the decisions on the ESM were taken, because I did not agree to approve it. But it was clear that Prime Minister Ratas would give the Council its approval either way. On the other hand, I repeatedly stressed both in the government and in the coalition council that our party does not support the ESM itself or the ESM reform, and this must also be taken into account in ratification. By the way, the representatives of our party in the European Union Affairs Committee also opposed the reform of the ESM when we were in the government. After all, our opposition is confirmed by the explanatory memorandum, which states discreetly that the Treaty amending the ESM Treaty was signed by the rest of the countries on 27 January, including Estonia on 8 February. These two different signing dates are there because Estonia's previous government had not given the mandate to sign. Thus, our party's position has been very consistent and clear, unambiguously negative with regard to the ESM at all times. We also opposed the resolution fund of the European Union Banking Union in 2016 when it reached the Riigikogu. This has not required Estonian taxpayers' money, unlike the ESM, but it is a scheme to channel money collected through the Guarantee Fund for the protection of Estonian depositors into a large pan-European pot. This is the scheme that collects EUR 60 to 70 billion across Europe and is sold to us with a story that, if necessary, it will also save Estonian banks. But we are realists, show me a person who speaks with a serious face that this money will eventually help the banks operating in Estonia, not some Mediterranean country or German and French banks, and I'll show you a fool out of your mouth. All the three drafts that we now have here in a row are dealing with the same thing, 479, 480 and 481, so it makes no sense for me to go over the same thing for all of them, and it is enough for me to make one speech now. This is a close, sometimes very technical and complex story for all these drafts, which is often written in the language of the European Union. It is therefore necessary that we speak in a very simple language which is wrong here. The wrong thing is, we're even in the ESM. The wrong thing is that we have poured the money from our bank depositors into the Big Peeter bag, listening to the fools talk, so we protect the Estonian depositors. It is also wrong that these two funds are now being put together and that the possibility of mobilising the funds in them has become considerably simpler and that the area of use has been broadened. Simply put, the terms of the very large-scale financial obligations accepted by the various solemn assurances are now amended ex post as if through the back door, retreating from those assurances and promises that were made at the time. This will make the purpose of using money more widely and simplify conditions. And we're talking about a lot of money. We are talking about the EUR 140 billion, perhaps even EUR 150 billion, of the ESM and the fund's money being put together. We are now talking about rescuing large European banks directly through the ESM with taxpayers' money. With our money. That's the whole point. It is also wrong that we see very serious problems piling up over the European economy and the world financial system. This reform is trying to keep the system running a little bit more on print money, less interest and a growing debt burden. Even if the current financial system were to collapse, 150 billion will, of course, not help anything, just that we have become a very large piece poorer. So that's also wrong. Even if we were in favour of this reform, it will not help in the event of a crisis, in the case of a really big crisis. No person with conscience can approve of this reform. Even if it's never used, he's basically wrong. Conservative People's Party proposes to reject the draft. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U16	[[Remote connection]] Thank you, Mr President of the Riigikogu. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Really, we're moving forward fast. With regard to the previous draft, I had the opportunity to present the amendment of the ESM Treaty due to the reform of the ESM, and I have already proposed ratifying the Treaty amending the ESM Treaty, and the draft that is currently before you is quite directly linked to the previous one. The Act on the ratification and implementation of the ESM Treaty, adopted by the Riigikogu in 2012, contains, as the name suggests, a number of implementing provisions on how the government and the Riigikogu cooperate on ESM issues. With regard to the amendment of the ESM Agreement, there is now a need to adjust and revise these procedures slightly. Overall, the cooperation on ESM has worked well so far, and the government's conviction is that it will continue to do so. I can also confirm that the Riigikogu remains involved in all important issues. In making our proposals, we in the government were based on the weight of a specific decision, in particular whether the decision has an impact on the obligations of the Estonian state, but also on confidentiality requirements and time criticality. Thus, only with the mandate given by the Riigikogu on the basis of the decision of the Riigikogu, Estonian representatives can vote on the boards of the ESM, firstly, when deciding on the takeover of the obligations of the EFSF and the issue of related shares, and secondly, when making the decision to launch the resolution fund's defence mechanism, as well as when setting the maximum limit for the resolution fund's defence mechanism. These decisions cannot be taken until the Riigikogu has approved them separately and given them a mandate. In the case of the introduction of ESM-preventive credit lines, the proposal is such that the Riigikogu's mandate could be given to the representatives of Estonia by the European Union Affairs Committee, as the terms and conditions of these instruments, as explained in the introduction of the previous draft ESM Agreement, will be standardised. In the case of a credit line with pre-conditions, the Memorandum of Understanding is no longer concluded and the content of the credit line with additional terms is limited and then it is simply not appropriate to take a full decision in the Riigikogu. Similarly, it is proposed that ELAK be a mandate giver if there is a situation where it is desirable to delegate some decisions relating to the implementation of the safeguard mechanism from the Board of Directors to the Executive Director. The implementation of the safeguard mechanism for specific resolution cases always requires very urgent decision-making, mostly within 12 hours. In addition, there are always very strict confidentiality requirements. If these decisions have not been delegated to the Executive Director or are outside this scope, we propose informing the Riigikogu of the decisions of the Board of Directors, written notification. These are proposals that the government considers balanced and ensure that the Riigikogu is informed and directly involved in making important decisions. On this draft too, I would like to thank the Committee on Monetary Affairs of the Riigikogu, which unanimously supported and supported the proposals as well. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U19	[[Remote connection]] Thank you. I see that Italy is really on your mind today. As the issue concerned a precautionary conditional credit line, despite the suspension of compliance with or the requirement to comply with the budget rules, these conditions must be met in order to qualify for the precautionary conditional credit line. Indeed, one of the conditions is that a specific ESM member who requests this assistance through a conditional credit line must not have been subject to an excessive deficit procedure, and this must have been the case in the past two years. And in the past two years, it must also meet certain criteria, such as a deficit not exceeding 3% of GDP. Similarly, the structural balance must be at or above a country-specific minimum level. There is also a debt criterion, i.e. the debt must not exceed 60% of GDP, i.e. the debt criterion must be fulfilled in the same way or it must move towards 60%. If these conditions are not met, this simply means that it is not possible to use the precautionary conditional credit line aid. It's as simple as that. The question also concerned the number of years in which Italy will reach compliance with the conditions listed in its budget. I'm gonna be honest with you. I can't really figure it out right now. The aim is to prevent and mitigate the anxiety that otherwise spreads very quickly in crisis situations, the anxiety that emerges in the markets. The resolution itself, the entire resolution system, the crisis action, the safeguard that the ESM will now play, is actually directly fulfilling this objective. It is inevitable that decisions must be taken very quickly in critical situations. The alternative would be that stability in the euro area as a whole is indeed at risk. It would be very difficult to say that if such a situation were to arise, Estonia would not be negatively affected. In any case, it would. Our interest is certainly that such a common crisis-landing system will work and be in place. I will take this opportunity to explain once more or to dispel what cases an ESM safeguard measure could be taken in the first place. This is not the case that it can be implemented at any given moment. The assumption that the Board can apply to the ESM at all is that all previous options have been exhausted. This means that the bank's own resources have been exhausted, that the possibility of using both the bank's shareholders and creditors has been exhausted, and that the crisis management fund itself has fully mobilised its own funds. Once all previous options have been exhausted, you can apply for a loan from the ESM. And there are limits ahead too. There, too, the total amount of loans granted cannot exceed the size of the resolution fund, estimated to be EUR 68 to 70 billion. In such a situation, where all previous measures have indeed exhausted themselves, the provision of such last aid or rescue straw is necessary in order to ensure overall stability. So we certainly disagree on this issue. I am deeply convinced that without such a safeguard mechanism, the situation would be much more fragile and the eurozone as a whole would be in a much more difficult situation.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U38	[[Remote connection]] Thank you, good Chairman of the Riigikogu! Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! This is the third draft of the ESM reform package. The amendments to the SRF contribution agreement are linked to the agreement amending the ESM, namely the launch of the Banking Union safeguard mechanism in 2022. The amendments to the agreement were signed by Estonia in February 2021 and in these negotiations too, the positions of the government and the Riigikogu have been guided over several years. Since 2015, banks have already made payments to the Single Resolution Fund (SRF). The fund has increased its share of the pool each year and the collection period, i.e. by the end of the transitional period, by the end of 2023, should have reached its target. It is 1% of covered deposits. As has been said, it is estimated at around EUR 70 billion. And indeed, at the end of this summer, the fund had accumulated some EUR 52 billion. From 1 January 2024, the Fund shall be 100% shared. This means that there is no longer a distinction between which country's banks have received funds and in which country they will be used for resolution. It's already in the contract and it's going to work like this. However, the amendment of the agreement is due to the fact that one element of the ESM reform, the safeguard mechanism of the Banking Union, is to be introduced before the end of the transitional period, i.e. early 2022. It is not yet intended to be used in any particular resolution case. This decision is not currently being taken, but it concerns, as explained in the ESM Treaty, the principle of acceptance of the broad framework of the safeguard mechanism. Should there ever be a situation in which the safeguard mechanism should actually apply to some resolution, the loans received from the Board and the resolution fund ESM will be repaid by way of ex post contributions from the banks. The current change in the contract concerns these payments. According to the current agreement, during the transitional period these payments cannot be redirected to the joint tranche, i.e. to the repayment of the loan. A change of contract will give rise to that possibility. In doing so, the payments to be collected would not go directly to 100% of the pool, but would start at 70% and grow by 3.75% each subsequent quarter, so that 100% would be reached by 2024 at the beginning of 2022. We have agreed to provide the ESM with a safeguard mechanism function and, at the end of 2020, the government also agreed with the assessment by the European Commission and the European Central Bank that the risks in the banking sector have been sufficiently reduced and the safeguard mechanism is feasible earlier than originally planned, i.e. before the end of the resolution fund's collection period in 2024. At the same time, the activation of the safeguard mechanism means that the States' preparedness loans to the Board will be cancelled. These contracts temporarily fulfilled the role of the defence mechanism of the resolution fund and Estonia had then, by decision of the Riigikogu, granted such a readiness loan of 22 million euros. In fact, it didn't make use of it. The same assumption and hope apply, of course, to the fact that the ESM safeguard mechanism does not need to be used in practice. The government's proposal is to ratify the amendments to the agreement on contributions to the resolution fund. Once again, I thank the Committee on Monetary Affairs, which has already unanimously supported it. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U103	Thank you for your question. Indeed, a large part of this question could be answered by the Minister for Foreign Affairs. If you are asking whether the border agreement between Estonia and the Russian Federation is important, then yes, it is important that there is a valid border agreement between Estonia and the Russian Federation. When we talk about today's temporary control line and we call it the external border, it is actually right to call it the temporary control line. This particular control line has been agreed, otherwise it would not be possible to carry out the planned border construction that we are about to do on our temporary control line. We have been doing this since the summer of 2020. Indeed, active activities are ongoing. If we had not agreed where the temporary check line was running, it would be very difficult for us to carry out the planned border construction, even less to place temporary delays on our external borders, which we are working with the Defence Forces to do in the event of additional training. Indeed, I say as an intermediate chord, I am extremely pleased that one of the objectives of the additional training gathering is to practice cooperation with the Police and Border Guard Board and, in the process, to establish temporary barriers to the border, precisely in those places where it is extremely important. When we're talking about border facilities, ready-to-be border gardens, temporary delay gardens, border guarding isn't just about these gardens. Border surveillance consists of three components: people, border guards, surveillance equipment and border infrastructure. By infrastructure, we mean a particular garden, but it includes patrol tracks and so on. From the perspective of border surveillance, these are very successful instruments and measures. Surveillance techniques give us an opportunity to detect and respond early, while the delay garden ensures that the reacting forces arrive quickly. In Estonia's legal space, border guards are indeed entitled to prevent illegal border crossings, including through coercion. No one has the right to illegally cross the external border; every country's sovereign right is to defend it. If we are talking about defending the border, or a situation where the border really needs to be protected, then of course we are also waiting for the defence forces to help. This is also one of the things we are practicing during the additional training. In short, border surveillance and border protection are somewhat different aspects. If the border needs to be protected, the defence forces will certainly come to our aid, and then there will be a completely different legal basis for vigorously defending our country.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U122	Thank you for the question. There was a lot of topic here, so I'll try to be as brief as possible. The administrative reform took place until 2017 and I would say "administrative territorial reform" because the functions of the municipalities did not change to a large extent, but in 2017 the government also decided to increase the revenue base of the municipalities considerably so that larger, more capable municipalities would receive more resources to organise local life. No such in-depth audit has been carried out directly. I know that the National Audit Office carried out an audit where it tried to evaluate the results of the reform from the point of view of peripheralisation. He chose ten municipalities, as far as I know, and assessed whether the trend of peripheralization in the new, larger municipalities would not get worse, or that the periphery of this larger government would be overlooked in different areas. The answer was, in general, no. At least in these ten municipalities, and I can assure you that even more broadly, this result has been even more positive. Very many of the investments that were not made in the smaller municipalities have now been merged, and it has been possible to do so in the larger municipalities. That is for two main reasons. [Firstly] the competences that exist in the municipalities have improved, i.e. officials and staff have a better competence. That's the analysis we did. While in the past there was one 0.2 [place] official in one field, 0.2 [place] in another, in the case of basic functions or basic services, there is now a competent full-time specialist in the municipalities. We have also launched a platform such as my own government.fin.ee, where each year we compare how advanced services, be they social services, education services, utilities services, have developed. There is a pretty good comparison of how this service has evolved over the years in a particular municipality, as well as a comparison with neighbouring municipalities and other municipalities. The overall trend is that these services are improving. I'm sure there's a lot to do. I have also been asked whether there will be a rapid return of some kind of national administrative reform towards the municipalities. I can say that neither the previous government coalition nor the current coalition have prepared or prepared any such reform. My proposal is to restore the merger grants from 2025, i.e. with regard to the following local authorities [elections]. Whether they come will be clear in the course of [making] the country's budget strategy in August.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U205	Thank you, Mr President. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Maybe with this bill, we'd go back a little bit in history. As of 1 July 2017, all persons covered by adult health insurance began to receive compensation at a limit of EUR 30 per year and EUR 40 per share as of 1 January 2018 and the share was 50% of the marginal price. At the same time, dental care for pensioners, people with special needs, pregnant women and mothers with small children increased from EUR 19 to EUR 85. The own contribution of these persons is 15% of the marginal price for services. What I'm going to say is that the compensation is in kind. The patient doesn't get the money, but it moves directly from the health insurance fund to the service provider. In 2018, the compensation was worth EUR 12 million. Maybe some people today will ask why they didn't do it right away and at once and more. Because then it was in the budget. The idea of social democracies in setting up dental benefits for adults was, and is, that annual dental benefits must increase and, at the same time, their own participation must decrease, also in the case of target groups receiving a higher threshold. Dental benefits have helped more people to see a dentist. The main beneficiaries of the benefit are pensioners and those with partial or lack of work capacity, as is clear from the analysis of the application of dental care benefits. After the entry into force of the adult dental care benefit in 2017, both the number of dental first visits, the number of more frequent visits to the doctor and the use of the benefit from year to year increased. The Social Democrats support every step taken to expand the range of people benefiting from the increased threshold, in other words, the unemployed and beneficiaries of income support also receive compensation at the increased threshold. Research shows that we still have too many people who do not take care of their oral hygiene and do so even when dental care is free. Studies have been carried out at different times, but the same picture is drawn. As a result, more emphasis must be placed on prevention, as it would help prevent or detect diseases early, and it is also important to put emphasis on communication, especially among children and young people. "The auditor's report points out that even preferential benefits do not improve the availability of dental care, as the protection of people with high medical needs from high healthcare costs remains very low and ineffective. In dental care, people's own costs are high, according to recent data, nearly three times higher than in healthcare in general. ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U241	Ladies and gentlemen, With this bill, it solves a number of technical problems. At first glance, it may seem as if this bill is satisfied and there are no problems. However, there is an important disadvantage here, and this could be called a fundamental, fundamental problem. We are talking about §8 (2) of the draft, which states that the charge for the use of infrastructure is set at 3%. This 3% reflects once again the indifference of the Estonian state and the superior attitude towards private property. It's sad we're going along with that in this hall. Private property is an important cornerstone of our country and § 32 of the Constitution says that everyone's property is untouchable and equally protected. In 2012, the Supreme Court declared the current law, which deprived owners of fair compensation when they were burdened with property, unconstitutional. It should be said that for the first time, the Supreme Court has declared this law unconstitutional as early as 2004. For many years, there was a debate and a debate on how much fair compensation should be. On 17 January 2018, the Riigikogu passed the Act on the Implementation of the Law of Property Act and on the amendment of other laws, which supplemented the Act with § 155, which says that the farm fee is 7.5%. Anyone who wishes to read more about this bill than the explanatory memorandum can read how the percentage was reached. Let it be said only briefly that 5% was intended for fair compensation and 2,5% for land tax. This Act entered into force on 1 January 2019. At the time, it was thought that the church was in the middle of the village, or different interests were balanced. Already then, it was said that there would be an assessment of the value of the land. Its preparation started in the middle of the last decade and, in this light, discussions were also held on the percentage that eventually entered the law, i.e. 7.5% already took this principle into account. In good faith, private owners made agreements in the following years to burden their property with infrastructure objects. We have now come to a situation where we say that the value of the land has increased too much, and therefore, good landowners, we are taking almost half of what you promised. Such conduct in a country that respects private property does not suffer any criticism. Let it be said that Riigi Kinnisvara AS pursues a return of almost 10% on its investments. That's normal. But in the case of a private owner, 5% is an obvious enrichment. Recently, Enefit Green was a stock issue. The stock price has increased a lot today. Is the next step a part of the renationalisation of shares because investors have earned too much? That is what the draft today contains. And it's a disgrace. Now for legal clarity. If we adopt this bill, we have two laws that deal differently with the same thing. What is the basis for the selection? How is a common man supposed to know when he can get 7.5% and when he can cross the 7.5% requirement and tell you that 3% is enough for you, you're already rich? The explanatory memorandum tries to do so in its legal pulpit and says that the law implementing the Law of Property Law will continue to be implemented in the country of income. Other destinations are expected to have a coefficient of 0, but can also be determined differently depending on the situation. How often does ordinary people read the explanatory notes of laws? And what things are these different situations... More time, please.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U242	Three more minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U243	What are the different situations when the coefficient can be greater than 0, and who determines these percentages then? Ladies and gentlemen, The Riigikogu Committee on Monetary Affairs discussed this draft in the preparation of the third reading at its meeting on 16 November. As far as the content of the draft is concerned, the draft has been drawn up in accordance with the State Budget Act, according to which the Government may initiate a draft law amending the state budget no later than two months before the end of the financial year, without changing the total amount of expenditure. And given that this year's State Budget Act has been drawn up last autumn and some funding needs have changed, it is appropriate to initiate a change in the state budget in order to improve the efficiency of the objectives set by the public authorities. No proposals were received from the members of the Riigikogu, political groups and committees by the deadline of 15 November at 14:00. The Finance Committee, in cooperation with the ministries, drafted an amendment specifying the costs of one of the 13 amendments and added a provision to enter into force on the day following the publication of the Act in the Official Journal of the State. Explanations of amendments are included in the list of amendments. And if you stay with them, the first concerns the transfer of funds from the Chancellery of the Riigikogu. One major change is under the Government of the Republic and concerns the distribution of the budget of the Ministry of Social Affairs' labour market programme to the expenses of the Government of the Republic's reserve of funds of EUR 46,7 million. Then there are the changes between the various programmes in the field of the Ministry of Justice. There are changes to the Department of Defense. The point was that it may have been in the spring or summer when we looked at the budget for this year on some subject, and then the speaker himself asked why the cost of allies' infrastructure in the area of the Ministry of Defence's administration was divided between all four different programmes, although the cost of infrastructure could be included in the area of collective protection in terms of its content. There was no reply immediately, a written inquiry had to be made. The written enquiry was answered in such a way that there was an error and that this error is being corrected. So I have heard here the accusation that if a member of the Riigikogu asks for anything about a specific item in the budget, he will receive an answer, but nothing will be done. Yes, they are. They just fixed one mistake. There are also changes in the area of government of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and in the area of government of the Ministry of Finance. And one of the changes concerned the area of government of the Ministry of the Interior: the re-introduction of budgetary resources in the area of government will amount to 4.6 million euros in respect of the requirements of national defence secrets from the budget for building a defence police building into the normal budget of the Defence Police. Now the debate in the Commission is also brief. Here's an extract from the November 16th protocol. First of all, we had questions answered by Regina Vällik, adviser to the Budget Department of the Ministry of Finance. Our debate was rather short. The speaker asked two questions. I asked for clarification of the amendment tabled by the Ministry of Justice concerning the breakdown of measures in the field of performance. The Commission subsequently received a written reply. And the speaker also asked a second question, which concerned the sum of the general investments presented in the form of state secrets by the Ministry of the Interior. The point is that this 4.6 million in the amendment was already allocated to the original supplementary budget and is now being moved to the section of state secret. I asked why it had to be done. The answer was.. I quote that the Ministry of the Interior has specified this amount in an official way, that it has identified the reasons for which the amount of investment was initially not presented as a state secret, and how to proceed in the future. The amount as such is not a state secret, but the whole further operation, which activities are carried out and how they are carried out, is treated as a state secret. And that is why it cannot be planned publicly in the budget if the implementation of the budget must be restricted to access. Such an explanation. And that was the end of our debate. The Commission made the following decisions consensually: first, to propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu on Wednesday, 24 November, then the third reading should be closed and the final vote carried out. A majority of the votes required for the adoption of the bill as a law. Thank you for your attention and I am ready to answer the questions.

All this brings me to the next few keywords today. First of all, I note that there is some room for improvement in resolving really large criminal proceedings quickly and at the same time in a high-quality manner. And one possible solution is to simplify the procedure for investigating evidence. This could be improved both by practice and by amending the law. Another keyword is public access to justice, both sessions and solutions and materials. This complex task, indeed, awaits the completion of the draft for some time, but until now, of course, this must be dealt with in practice. As with freedom always comes with responsibility, it must be remembered that this issue must go hand in hand with respect for the presumption of innocence. However, access to justice is never complete until the decisions and the work of the courts are understandable. They have to be. The judges must be prepared to explain their decisions in the human language and must also strive to ensure that the reasonings already contained in the judgments are in normal Estonian language and can be monitored without the help of a lawyer. I understand the Constitution in such a way that I am responsible for the preservation of the Estonian language as a lifelong servant of the Estonian state. And, of course, people are important. No one should feel like a second-rate person next to the so-called first-class people. The role of the Court as guarantor of fundamental rights enshrined in the Constitution cannot be underestimated. If a court must be harsh because the law obliges it to do so, it must be. But in criminal proceedings, there must be no impression that a court is superior to a person or that an individual is expected to comply with a better law than the state itself. If that were to happen, it would show the state's penal power to be arbitrary and unjust. Criminal proceedings must strive for both seeming and actual justice, and one of the pillars of this is equality between the parties. In a legal system where a substantial number of criminal cases are resolved in settlement proceedings, it is particularly important that both our prosecutors and lawyers, each of whom plays their part, can be trusted. And one such guarantee of certainty is to ensure the confidentiality of communication between the lawyer and the client, if one example is given. Standards for searches at law firms have been one of the concerns I have deliberately dealt with both as a practitioner and in writing legal literature, which is why last week's human rights court decision did not come as a surprise to me. Those were some of the keywords I mentioned under the pressure of time. Almost five years ago, I was standing right here hoping for your support in another profession, and I assured you that there is nothing more natural for me than to serve the Estonian state. I'll confirm that today. Thank you very much for listening, and if you have any questions, I am very happy to answer.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U358	Our 16th item is the second reading of the Bill 368 of Amendment to the Copyright Act (transposition of the Copyright Directives) initiated by the Government of the Republic. By the Committee on Culture, Üllar Saaremäe presents the draft. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U359	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Dear Riigikogu! I'm in front of you with 368 SE's second reading. A bit of history, too, because this is a bill that has been in the process for quite a while and perhaps even the reasons why we are dealing with it have already been forgotten. The draft law amending the Copyright Act was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 5 April 2021. The draft amends the Copyright Act by virtue of two EU directives: the DSM Directive and the so-called "Kab-Sat2 Directive." The objectives of the DSM Directive are to enable greater public access to and access to digital content online and across borders; secondly, to clarify the rules on free use for bodies in the fields of research, education and cultural heritage; and thirdly, to provide a fair and sustainable market for artists, creative industries and the media. The Kab-Sat2 Directive amends the rules on the exercise of copyright and related rights applicable to broadcasters, certain web broadcasts and the retransmission of television and radio broadcasts in order to promote the dissemination of programmes and thus improve the availability of content, and also across borders. The first reading of the draft was completed on 5 May this year. No amendments were tabled by the deadline of 19 May. The Committee on Culture requested the views of stakeholders involved in the preparation of the draft by 3 May. The opinion of the Cultural Commission was presented by the Estonian Authors' Association, the Estonian Artists' Association, the Estonian Foundation of Phonogram Producers' Association, the Estonian Film Industry Cluster, the Estonian Film Operators' Association, the Estonian Publishers' Association, Facebook, Google, the Tallinn Central Library, the Estonian Information Technology and Telecommunications Association and the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry. In preparation for the second reading of the bill, the Commission also received additional requests from the Tallinn Central Library, the Estonian Publishers' Association and the Estonian Fonogram Manufacturers' Association. The Commission prepared the draft for a second reading at the following meetings: 1 and 15 June, 21 September, 19 October and 16 and 22 November. In preparation for the second reading of the draft, the Committee on Culture discussed on 1 June, together with stakeholders' representatives and the Ministry of Justice, the proposals put forward by stakeholders. During the meeting, the discussion of the Committee on Culture focused on the issues raised by stakeholders. Firstly, [we dealt] with the obligation to notify the author's agreement, including the amendment of the author's agreement, the termination or withdrawal of the author's agreement. If there are interest groups on this or that side of the screen, uncrypto [attended § 491]. Secondly, [We] dealt with the liability of the platform provider in communicating the subject of rights to the public and making it available to the public, [Added § 571], additional terms such as "educational institution," "motivated volume," "Illustrative material," definition in the Copyright Act, the scope of the exemption for education in the field of free use of works in education and cultural heritage institutions, and the use of the object of non-commercial rights in cultural heritage institutions. And fourthly, we addressed the proposal of film operators to switch to the so-called contractual model and to abolish the legal transition of property rights, which is the basis of the current practice. Unfortunately, this latter cannot be corrected by these directives. The responses of the initiator of the draft act to interest groups and these proposals are available on the Riigikogu website. I'm not going to read them out here for a while. On 15 June, 21 September and 19 October, the Committee on Culture discussed as an important topic the proposal of the Estonian Artists' Association to define the concept of an educational institution in § [19] of the Copyright Act and to make terminological clarifications in the Act, to replace the "learning objective" with the "education objective." The Committee on Culture accompanied the Ministry of Education and Research on these issues, who presented a written statement to the Commission and explained that although grammarly the concept of an educational institution is somewhat broader than that of an educational institution, it does not change the situation that the educational institution is primarily meant to include educational institutions where learning takes place and learning according to the curriculum registered in the Estonian Education Information System. With regard to the term "educational objective" of the Copyright Act, the Ministry of Justice further stated that without an in-depth analysis, it is not clear what impact such a substitution could have. The Ministry of Justice will therefore commission a study on the practical importance of educational exceptions in the conduct of studies. This would help to assess the impact of these exceptions on rightholders. According to these answers, the Committee on Culture did not initiate amendments on the basis of the positions of the ministries. On 15 June and 19 October, the Committee on Culture discussed copyright regulation as one of the main topics which provides a framework for assessing which work or subject matter of related rights can be considered to be out of trade and therefore easier to use by cultural heritage institutions. Paragraph 572 (1) of the Copyright Act is a general rule. In the case of objects covered by copyright, the cultural heritage authority must make a reasonable effort to know whether the work, the object of the rights, is in trade turnover. In addition to this general assumption, a somewhat more favourable regime has been created in subsections 3 to 7 of the same section. In accordance with paragraph 3, a work, or object of rights, which is protected by copyright and is created at least 50 years before its use, shall no longer be subject to trade turnover unless there are obvious circumstances which preclude it. By obvious circumstances, we can actually consider that it cannot be known to the cultural heritage institution that the work is still being marketed, marketed or sold, or available on platforms somewhere. The Association of Estonian Publishers proposed to exclude books on the application of paragraph 3. The Committee on Culture accompanied this topic to the debate of the Ministry of Culture, which, together with the Ministry of Justice, submitted a written position to the Commission. The ministries admitted that due to the new regulation the burden on rightholders would increase somewhat. At the same time, the possibility remains for rightholders to exclude at any time that the object of the rights created by them will be used on the basis of a new mechanism created by law. Perhaps in plain language, the author can always refuse to grant permission to use what he has done. The section on books was also clarified at the hearing. It was mentioned that the National Library allows lists of works for which they have a potential interest to be placed on its website and only then will they be included in the EUIPO database, the European Union Intellectual Property Agency. The amendment was also not initiated on the basis of these explanations. On 16 November, the Committee on Culture discussed the opinion of the Association of Estonian Phonogram Producers. The main issue raised by the association was that there is too little time for authors and performers to be informed. It is known that the law should have entered into force on 7 June 2021, and now this information obligation should have come into force [a year later], leaving them only 5 to 6 months [to bring their activities into line with the law]. In the Committee on Culture, a representative of the Ministry of Justice explained that a more flexible interpretation of [the provision] is currently possible. In carrying out these duties, account shall be taken, as far as possible, of the different practices and practices of the profession or profession. As a result, it would be possible for the debtor to be able to rely on the fact that the provision of information is possible once a year, also taking into account the duration of the financial year and the resulting reporting period, in the event of the performance of the obligation, which would be 7 June 2022. Therefore, both 2021 and 2022 can be reported simultaneously by the obliged party at the beginning of 2023. All transitional provisions in the Copyright Act, including those concerning the obligation of authors and performers to notify, have been left unchanged in the draft. According to the Ministry of Justice, the deadlines deriving directly from the Directive cannot be changed, as that is the position of the European Commission. At its meeting on 16 November, it was decided that the Committee on Culture should submit amendments and that procedural decisions were taken. Three amendments were tabled by the Committee on Culture. The necessary linguistic and technical changes were introduced in the draft. The changes made to the text of the bill are underlined by this I say to the interested parties. The Committee on Culture's amendments are also supported by the Ministry of Justice as the initiator of the draft. I am very sorry that I am telling you all this for so long, but since it was six meetings, it is not possible to bring them together in a few words. And now a few words about the amendments. The purpose of the first amendment is to point out, for the sake of clarity, that § 579 (3) and § 5710 (1) of the Copyright Act contain cumulative, not alternative, obligations on platform providers. In other words, if the platform provider demonstrates that it has taken all the measures mentioned in the above paragraphs, the platform provider shall not be liable for the unauthorised disclosure by the user of his service of the object of rights to the public or for making it available to the public via the platform provider's website. This was fully taken into account by the Committee on Culture. The purpose of the second amendment is to formulate a more precise obligation on platform providers to improve legal clarity. The amendment is based on a proposal from interest groups, namely the Estonian Artists' Association, the Estonian Foundation of Phonogram Producers and the Estonian Authors' Association. In short, as a result of this amendment, it is clear that a platform provider must take measures to fulfil its obligations in order not to block but to allow legitimate uploads. So it's for direct user protection. The Committee on Culture has taken full account of this proposal. Third amendment: delete § 2 from [the draft law]. § 2 of the Bill is the original time of entry into force of the Act, which has passed. It should have been 7 June 2021. As a result, the law will enter into force in general terms, i.e. on the 10th day following its publication in the Official Journal of the State. The Committee on Culture took full account of this. And now, finally, the procedural decisions and proposals of the commission. The Committee on Culture proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for a second reading. a on 24 November 2010 a proposal to conclude the second reading. When the second reading of the draft is closed, the Commission's proposal to put the draft on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for the third reading is on 8 December this year and then to hold a final vote. All procedural decisions were consensual. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U389	Good evening, ladies and gentlemen. First of all, I would like to remind you briefly that the content of the draft amendment to the Maritime Safety Act, 455, is a 50% reduction in the fee due for water from January to December 2022. The draft passed the first reading in the Riigikogu on 27 October this year. The Committee on Economic Affairs discussed the draft for second reading first of all on 8 November this year. Jaak Viilius, Head of the Maritime Economics Department of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, introduced support measures for fishing vessels in connection with COVID more widely than envisaged in the draft, because the Estonian Shipowners' Association had sent a letter to the Riigikogu and asked for funds to be allocated to continue the special regime of passenger ships in 2022. The special regime for passenger ships has been introduced by the Government of the Republic Regulation 57, but resources should be provided for this in the 2022 budget. And there are two measures. Let us start with a reduction in the fees for waterways, which is the content of this draft. All vessels were exempted from the fees from 1 April 2020 until 31 March 2021. From 1 April to 31 December this year, a reduced rate of 50% will apply to all ships entering Estonian ports or ports. The pre-crisis waterway charge was €18 million, including a passenger ship share of just under 50%. In this respect, it is important to note that due to the reduction of water prices, the volume of goods in the Port of Tallinn alone increased by 7%, while the total volume of goods in the Baltic Sea region decreased by 7.9%. It is worth taking a little break and thinking that suddenly Estonian waterway fees were a little high in the Baltic Sea competition even before the COVID crisis and its effects arrived. The second measure, the special regime for passenger ships, means the refund of labour taxes on the basis of income and social contributions paid in Estonia and on the basis of the tax paid by members of the crew, which is the 2.5-fold difference between the minimum wage calculated for ratings 1,5 and the minimum wage calculated for managers. The introduction of the special regime has been authorised by State aid until 2023. It is estimated that the measure will have an impact on 3000 jobs on ships and 100 jobs on shore, mainly in four companies. Moreover, these ships serve routes between Estonia and Finland and Sweden. These countries are known to be Estonia's largest trading partners, including 25% of Estonia's exports to these countries. Therefore, support measures for shipping are not only measures for shipping, but support measures for the Estonian economy as a whole and their importance cannot be underestimated. EUR 3,7 million was disbursed in 2020 and EUR 7,7 million is planned for 2021. According to the Department of Transport, they may be exhausted by the end of the year and the last part of the aid must be paid out proportionally. This is the overview provided by Jaak Viilitus to the Commission on 8 November. By initiating the draft, the government envisaged a 25% reduction in the fee for waterways. The EKRE Group has proposed an amendment to the draft to reduce the waterway charge by 50%. On 8 November, the Commission's advisor stated that the amendment proposed by the EKRE meant whether the first paragraph of the draft was taken into account. The draft contains two paragraphs. Mr Breivel confirmed that the amendment did indeed concern the first paragraph. It was decided to continue the discussion at its meeting on 9 November. Two amendments were considered on 9 November. The first was the amendment tabled by the EKRE on the 50% reduction in the fee for waterways, which was taken into account in substance. The second amendment was an amendment by the commission, which was correctly formulated, as the amendment by the EER had to be corrected initially. The amendment proposed by the commission was to replace the part of the text in § 1 by "25 per cent" by "50 per cent," which was fully taken into account by the commission. Further procedural decisions were taken on 9 November. Firstly, to put the draft on the plenary agenda by today, 24 November. Secondly, propose that the second reading be stopped. Third, when the second reading is closed, it is proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda and that the final vote be taken on 7 December 2021. The decisions were taken by consensus. The decision was made by Riho Breivel, Jüri Jaanson, Siim Kallas, Mikhail Korb, Kristen Michal, Sven Sester, Mikhail Stalnukhin and the speaker here. In the meantime, the circumstances changed, which is why the Committee on Economic Affairs discussed the draft once again at its meeting on 22 November. Minister Taavi Aas stated that due to the lack of budgetary resources, the reduction in the fee for waterways should be limited to 45%. Such amendments have been sent to the Chamber and you have them. The point one is the amendment proposed by the EERE with the revised wording to reduce the waterway charge by 50%, and the second is the proposal of the commission, the Committee on Economic Affairs, to reduce the rate of the waterway charge by 45%. The government did indeed work very hard to find budgetary resources, both to reduce the waterway charge to 50% and to continue the special regime for passenger ships until the end of 2022. As a result, the Ministry of Economic Affairs announced yesterday that the financial cover had been found for this purpose. I ask that we vote on both proposals and support the proposal of the Group of the Estonian Conservative People's Party to reduce the part of the water fee due by 50% for January to December 2022. The other one proposed by the Committee on Economic Affairs to reduce by 45% rather than to support it. Such serious work is being done in finding these funds in the ministries of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Ministry of Finance, in our Committee on Economic Affairs and in our colleagues in the Committee on Monetary Affairs. Yes, it must be said that there is cooperation between the coalition and the opposition. Of course, this draft is linked to the state budget and the opposition must now support the adoption of the 2022 budget in addition to implementing these good decisions. That's all I got.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U474	Thank you, good manager. Ladies and gentlemen, On 16 November, the Finance Committee discussed the preparation for second reading of the draft law amending the Income Tax Act 469. The purpose of the draft is to exempt persons of pensionable age from income tax at the level of the average old-age pension in such a way that, instead of the general exemption, the old-age pension age receives tax-free income up to the average old-age pension, regardless of the amount of its income. The draft also provides for the exemption of compulsory and supplementary funded pension payments from income tax for persons who have been assigned a lack of working capacity before their retirement age. Three amendments were tabled by the deadline of 10 November. The Committee on Finance also addressed five market participants and interest groups, but there were no comments from them. The amendments were examined by the Committee on Finance at its meeting on 16 November. One proposal was from the Social Democratic Party and two were from the Fatherland Party. The coalition's votes did not support these proposals. The Commission proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda by 24 November, that the second reading be closed, and that when the second reading is closed, that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda and the final vote be taken.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U563	Today's item is the draft law amending the State Pension Insurance Act and the Pension Pensions Act initiated by the Government of the Republic, 475 and the Minister of Social Protection Signe Riisalo presents it on behalf of the Government. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U564	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! The second draft seems more complicated, but it is much more technical and practical. First of all, it is a change of two laws. The aim is largely operational: helping both pension applicants and those applying the law to understand the legal provisions in the same way and in a simpler way at the Social Insurance Board. The second amendment that comes with the draft is the automatic continuation of payment in September for survivors' pensions who are 18 to 24 years old. Here the Riigikogu should already have a familiar background related to the education information system. But then we're gonna start making the changes that we're about to make. First of all, the amendments to the State Pension Insurance Act are the payment of survivor's pensions, as stated, to students aged 18 to 24 in September. Today, the payments to learners will be cut off in September. When data from the Ministry of Education and Research's information system arrives in October, it will be paid afterwards for September. The amendment provides that we will automatically pay this September. If the corresponding registry notification comes in October, will continue to be paid, or if this message does not come, i.e. the learning process has been completed, the person has won it for one month in September, but the state will still take it on to make the situation of the people better. Maybe we'll go back to EHIS data, but a month later. This amount in terms of expenditure on the state budget is actually quite small, EUR 20000, and it is estimated that we are talking about 1800 people, for whom this monthly income, including in September, is actually important. The second amendment, which must be mentioned, is the reduction in the entitlement to a survivor's pension. We have a reduced seniority requirement for people with reduced working capacity when determining old-age pensions, with a reduction of one year per three years. This draft proposal proposes to introduce the same benefit for survivors' pensions for periods in which the provider whose income is the subject of this calculation is partially or totally absent. If such a change were to take effect, the Social Insurance Board would check all the pension files, they would make these changes themselves, no additional obligations for people, no requests or any other administrative steps would be added. Now, the implementation of pension supplements for calculating a flexible old-age pension. Pension supplements are not counted today and are paid separately. It is proposed that pension supplements should also be taken into account when increasing and reducing pensions. The new flexible pension arrangements will then be implemented. What's in the background? The background is that if the amount of a flexible pension is fixed in advance, if the amount of a flexible pension is reduced in a clear way when the person retires earlier, the five years that each of the flexible pensions is allowed under the law in force, then the amount of the pension will also be included in that amount. It will then also be covered by this reduction coefficient. The amount of flexible pension in the case of an average old-age pension before retirement would be EUR 548 for one child. If you now retire five years earlier, the reduction in your flexible old-age pension would be 22.6%, which would make this person's pension EUR 449.37. If we add and then reduce the supplement to the child [to be paid for raising], the old age pension will be EUR 443,67 and the loss would be EUR 5,7. Against this background, of course, there is also the desire for people to stay on the labour market longer, not to withdraw this flexible pension from the past if their health and other living conditions allow it. However, the amount of pension at a later retirement would again be higher and would be 9 euros higher. If we include the pension supplements there, it would be EUR 777,29 and EUR 9 would be a win for a person. The next round is to clarify the procedure for the recalculation of pensions. At present, a recalculation is made either from the date the application was submitted or from the first date of the month following the month in which the application was submitted. As there have been differences of opinion in practice between both the applicant and the implementing body, for reasons of legal clarity, the pension is recalculated from the date of application. Again, so that it is not possible to keep these procedures for too long and to be equally understandable. Now, the distribution of benefits among those who raised children. In the absence of an agreement, where entitlement to an old-age pension, to a retirement pension and to a supplementary pension for raising children is granted, all persons entitled shall be equally divided. Such persons may be, in addition to parents, for example, the parent's spouse and others, not just the parents. The decision to grant pensions will now be made by sharing equally those child-related supplements when there are two separate applicants. Okay. Then, separately, we will also have the costs of the law relating to IT costs, which is EUR 20000, in addition to the cost of the survivor's pension before, as well as EUR 20000. The second law that we amend within this framework is the law on funded pensions. [Provides] that regardless of age, persons with a lack of working capacity can retire from the second pillar. If such a person has not yet reached the age of the second pillar pension but has regained his or her capacity for work, the bill will allow him or her to start collecting money again in the second pillar if he or she so wishes. In the case of a fixed-term or life-long pension, the re-start of the accumulation requires the suspension of the pension. The draft complements the regulation of the pension contract so that there is also the possibility of suspending the payment of the pension so that the person can continue to collect the pension in a simpler way if he or she has a temporary incapacity for work. This draft would enter into force on 1 January 2023. That kind of thing.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U579	Our 24th item is the draft law amending the State Secret and classified foreign information law and the Public Information Act 410 initiated by the Government of the Republic. And now we have the speech chair that Minister of Justice Maris Lauri is expecting. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U580	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I will introduce you to the draft law amending the State Secrets and the Secret Foreign Information Act and the Public Information Act 410. The purpose of this draft is to update the regulation of the State Secrets and the Secret Foreign Information Act. The general purpose of this Act is to ensure the security and external communication of the State by protecting classified information from disclosure and disclosure to unauthorised persons. The current law entered into force at the beginning of 2008. All the changes so far have been based on the need to improve regulation on individual issues. This draft brings together issues raised by state secrets and external classified information processors and authorities responsible for protecting classified information over the last few years and therefore has a rather broad range of topics. The draft was drawn up in cooperation with the Ministry of Justice, the Ministry of Defence, the Ministry of the Interior, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the State Chancellery, the Defence Police Department, the Foreign Intelligence Board and the Defence Forces in 2019 Now, about the issues we're dealing with. The first major set of issues relates to the general need to update the requirements for the processing of classified information. The draft establishes a regulation that follows the principles of modern information management and paperless procedures, including modern ways of processing information and information media. In particular, the current requirements for the processing of classified information have been drawn up in the light of paper documents and are therefore not fully and reasonably applicable to classified information processed electronically. To address this problem, for example, the rules on the labelling and destruction of classified information will be updated to meet the requirements of the Archives Act. At the level of the Government of the Republic regulation, the recording of classified information material and the control of the entity are regulated in more detail. For clarification: The presence and integrity of classified information media shall be reviewed regularly during the status check. The basis and time limits for the classification of information will also be improved. This is done on a proposal from the Ministry of the Interior and the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. For example, according to the bill, the Police and Border Guard Board is able to classify K Command's armaments, ammunition and special equipment as a limited state secret for up to 25 years. The draft extends the decision-making powers of the creators of classified information both in the early deletion of the information and in the extension of the classification period. In the interests of the efficiency of the decision-making process, the Minister can, according to the draft, delegate to the heads of the institution, where appropriate, the decision on the early deletion of information created within the scope of the ministry's administration. Under the current law, these decisions must be taken by the Minister. In this way, the head of the security authority, the Defence Forces and the Surveillance Authority shall have the right to decide on the early deletion of the state secret established by their own authority. In view of the need to know, the right to decide on the need for an extension of the period of secrecy will also be obtained by the Minister responsible for the field and, in the case of the State Chancellery, by the State Secretary. In accordance with the same objectives, the Head of the Security Authority shall decide on the extension of the period of secrecy established by the Security Authority. If the information is submitted to the Minister for decision, then the extension can only be decided by the Minister, not by the Authority. In the case of both early deletion and renewal, the rule applies that the Government of the Republic, as a collegial body, decides on the information that has been transferred to the information medium submitted to the Government of the Republic or to the Government Committee for decision-making. The draft will make it more flexible to grant the right to access a limited level of state secret to natural persons outside the Ministry's administration. The Minister shall have the opportunity to decide on a limited level of state secret for access to the right of natural persons outside the ministry's jurisdiction, if necessary by delegation to the Head of the Ministry's Administration. Under the current regulation, it is only the right of the Minister. For example, the Minister of the Interior must now grant access rights to cadets studying at the Academy of Internal Defence who need information in their studies. After the draft, these decisions can also be made, for example, by the Rector of the National Defence Academy, if the Minister of the Interior so decides. The objective of the draft act, which is very important in the field of defence, is to provide for the granting of the right of access to state secrets to a person in reserve who has been appointed to a military post, the performance of which necessarily requires the possession of a right of access to state secrets at a confidential level in the fields of defence and infrastructure and information protection. The draft establishes a security clearance procedure as soon as possible to grant access to such state secrets in order to ensure the functioning of military defence in preparation for situations (e.g. educational gatherings) and in real-world situations (e.g. martial law, mobilisation). The problems have started in practice with the fact that the person in the reserve does not have a daily service relationship with the Defence Forces. Therefore, the normal decision-making process for granting access has proved too time-consuming in practice. Prior to receiving the right of access to a confidential level of state secret, the person must be subject to a security check by which the person should submit the request himself or herself. However, the Defence Forces decide on the appointment of a military term, mostly on the basis of a person's training. According to the draft, the right to submit an application for inspection is granted to the Defence Forces, which evaluates the need to include and control a person in a particular military post, while maintaining the obligation to inform the person of his or her control and to ascertain the will of the person. This would be the case for a study gathering. The person in the reserve shall be informed at least 120 days in advance of the invitation to attend the gatherings. In the event of crises, for example, where mobilisation is required, martial law or increased willingness to defend, but also in the case of monitoring mobilisation readiness, for example, when additional training is collected, security checks can be carried out where necessary without prior verification of the individual's will. The checks themselves will be carried out in a faster procedure than has been the case. The draft complements the grounds for refusing to grant an authorisation for access to state secrets in accordance with the needs identified in the implementing practice. The requirements of the NATO Staff Trust Directive are part of this model. For example, authorisation can be refused to a person who has repeatedly violated the requirements for the protection of classified information. The basis for refusal associated with diagnosed addiction, be it narcotics, alcohol or gambling, has also been improved. The requirement to inform the person who carried out the security check of the change of name without delay shall be revoked. The information reaches the security authority through a registry request and therefore there is no need for the person to report the information quickly, and it can be obtained without the person's active participation. The draft extends the list of posts on which security checks on working persons are carried out by the Foreign Intelligence Agency. Under current law, it's the kapo. For example, in the interests of the objectivity and impartiality of the control, it is necessary that persons in positions in the Ministry of the Interior who coordinate the work of the kapo do so by the Foreign Intelligence Office. The proposal updates and extends the regulation on the transfer of state secrets outside the country. In the current law, the circle of authorities with the right to provide information is very narrow. Only the State Chancellery, the Ministry of Defence, the Ministry of Interior, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the Defence Forces and the Security Authority may pass on state secrets to an institution created by a foreign state, an international organisation or an international agreement. In view of Estonia's membership in the European Union, NATO and other international organisations, such a restriction is outdated and does not allow other ministries and public authorities, i.e. not listed, to perform their duties reasonably in relation to participation in the work of international organisations. The draft has created flexible regulation and unambiguous conditions for the transfer of information to a foreign country. The most important requirement under the principle of information protection is that the recipient of the information ensure protection against disclosure of the information transmitted. This requirement is expressed in external agreements, namely agreements on the exchange and protection of classified information, which the Republic of Estonia has concluded with foreign countries. If there is no such agreement yet, for example, as negotiations are still ongoing, information can also be provided on the basis of the decision of the Security Committee of the Government of the Republic if the recipient of the information ensures protection against disclosure of the information transmitted. The draft introduces one amendment to the Public Information Act on the proposal of an authorised representative for national security. The amendment concerns external information with access restrictions and aims to ensure greater legal clarity in the implementation of the requirements for the protection of external information with access restrictions. The Public Information Act does not currently have the possibility to make an AK mark in accordance with Estonian law for external information with a restriction on access. However, this information must be protected in practice as AK information, as it is neither public nor external classified information. For this reason, the current regulation creates confusion in the data processors, it is not unambiguous to understand which external information with a restriction on access and for what period must be treated as a document intended for official use. According to the draft, the possibility of using external information subject to a restriction on access is created, i.e. not an obligation for institutions that process such information, if necessary, also for internal use, if this is necessary in the interests of legal clarity by the information processor. Thank you.
