ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U661	Our next item on the agenda, 25., is the draft law amending the Act of the State Government on the Law of the Performance Institution and the Law of the National Opera, number 419. On behalf of the Government, this will be introduced by Tiit Terik, Minister of Culture. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U662	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! I bring before you a draft law which, in the best sense of the author of the draft, would create clearer conditions for supporting Estonia's diverse theatre image and would make the organisation and financing of the field more up-to-date. The aim of cutting red tape is also not of minor importance. The law of the performing institution in its current form is, in our opinion, in the way of time. In particular, the Act of a performing institution, which has been in force since 2003, and which has largely been based on its version since 1997, has so far regulated the activities of the performing institutions, in particular those acting as public authorities. At the same time, the activities of all state performing bodies have now been completed and there are no plans to form more. Instead, foundations created by the state have come and we also have a very diverse and thriving landscape of private performing institutions. Yes, it must be acknowledged that the landscape of the performing institutions has developed much faster in recent years than the legal space supporting it. The law that is still in force has also provided an opportunity for ambiguities, and there have also been litigation in the financing and implementation of the institutions, which is not, I believe, in any way to one or the other party to the dispute. The aim of amending the law is to make the legislation relating to performing institutions and performance activities clearer and more flexible. Firstly, we want to update the definition of performing institutions and other things that have changed in practice in the field over time. Secondly, we would reduce the bureaucracy and administrative burden on the performance agencies. Thirdly, we would organise a system of funding in the field of performing arts. With the exception of some important but still ancillary clauses, we could say that the performing institution will in future be a legal person or institution whose main activity is the stage and performance of theatre and dance performances or concerts, musical stage activities or the ancillary activities of performing arts. Of course, further clarifications and requirements would apply to those performing bodies that would like to continue to apply for public support in the future. In the current situation, all performing institutions are obliged to report themselves to the Ministry, but in the future, the creation of a new show agency should not necessarily be notified to the State. This is, I believe, also a simplification that could be justified in the field. In the case of state foundations, the rules of operation are largely laid down in the articles of association, and here the rules are also considerably simpler than the existing rules. On the side of bureaucracy, for example, the obligation to set up a Creative Council for all performing institutions so far will be removed, and performance agencies will have the opportunity to enter into contractual relations with creative workers other than only a specific contractual relationship. I believe that this will give the theatre a wider opportunity to formalise their contracts. We would also, in our view, simplify issues relating to the recording and archiving of performances, obviously with regard to the development and simplification of the corresponding technical means. As regards the funding of performing arts, we propose to the honourable Riigikogu that in the case of foundations established by the state, we can rely on the objectives set out in the statutes and what the founder has set as expectations, and in the case of private and municipal institutions, state money can be applied for from the state budget and, on the recommendation of the Commission, allocated on the basis of criteria to ensure the diversity of the field of performing arts and regional accessibility of performances. This is an overview of the monetary framework. The types of grants to be granted to the performing bodies, the procedures for applying and the conditions for the assessment and designation of applicants would be laid down in a draft decree of the Minister responsible. For a more precise organisation of the sector, the aim of bringing the ministerial regulation to the level is operationality and flexibility, as the organisations involved in the field see that there are reasons to make changes, we should not always come to Parliament with these questions in order to amend the law. I would like to highlight one more important issue concerning accessibility. For those who are not directly concerned, this may seem to be an empty matter, but according to the draft law, there would now be a new obligation on the part of the performing institutions, namely to inform access via their information channels. This means that people with special needs should be able to assess their situation in terms of accessibility when they come to watch a show or some other activity. If accessibility is not guaranteed, it must simply be communicated so that people can make their plans. It is a small step, but I believe it is a practical and important step that could be of interest in this area. The new act of a performing institution is in line with the landscape of performing arts and we believe that it promotes fresh creative ideas, ways of operating and, of course, theatre availability in all regions of Estonia. As regards the law of the National Opera in this same draft, there is one technical amendment: improving the outdated publication mark. I would like to thank the honourable members of the Riigikogu for the cooperation that has been involved in drafting the draft. I'd be happy to answer your questions. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U687	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Dear Riigikogu! Today, on several occasions, we have been called upon to describe what happened in the Commission in our own words. Well, that's not possible, because right now I have to go to the dialogue and it's full of pure gold, with my own words, you don't talk about it. The preparation of the draft Act amending the Law of the Performance Institution and the National Opera Act for the first reading took place on Tuesday, 12 October. Aadu Must and members of the committee Signe Kivi, Viktoria Ladynskaja-Kubits, Eduard Odines, Heidy Purga, Üllar Saaremäe, Kristina Šmigun-Vähi and Marko Šorin took part. Anneli Ott, Minister of Culture, Daniel Raudsepp, Deputy Chancellor of Arts of the Ministry of Culture, Karin Ligi, Legal Adviser to the Legal and Property Management Department, Theatre Adviser to the Arts Departments Laur Kaunissaare, and Advisers to the Minister Marta-Magdaleen King and Katarina Buddrik. The Minister of Culture said, as an introduction, that the aim of amending the Act on Performance Institutions is greater clarity and flexibility in the laws governing the field of performing arts. He also introduced changes, just as a moment ago the new Minister of Culture did before us today. Üllar Saaremäe asked for clarification on the abolition of the obligation to set up a creative council in force so far. Daniel Raudsepp explained that the obligation to set up a Creative Council would disappear because it is not practical for small performing institutions, especially private entities, to form a creative council, but if larger performing bodies want to own a Creative Council, it is not an obstacle, it is not a ban, and the Creative Council can still be formed. Heidy Purga referred to the Minister's regulation on the financing system, which was also mentioned on several occasions a moment ago, and asked whether the change of minister or political attraction could in this case endanger smaller performance institutions. Anneli Ott explained that the restrictive nature of the financing system in the current law is the existence of only one criterion, which creates a forced order. The flexibility of the regulation provides the direction of the most expected output from the performing institutions. The Minister agreed that there was always a political question or a nuance, but the level of the regulation is, in his opinion, better than the level of the law, for which it is not possible to react quickly as necessary. Daniel Raudsepp added that life is changing faster than the laws. The field of performing arts is very strong and there are also very strong representative organisations. In addition, the independent funding of the Cultural Endowment of Estonia ensures stability, especially in the field of private performance institutions. Laur Kaunissaare added that there is much more flexibility to gain than to lose the theoretical risks. At present, support for private performing institutions is also provided by a ministerial decree, but he also stated that there is a theoretical risk. Anneli Ott added that it is important to give the Committee on Monetary Affairs a broader set of criteria for evaluation, which will certainly add to diversity. Signe Kivi referred to Article 5 (1) of the Act of the performing institution, which states that the head of the performing institution shall ensure the performance of the functions of the performing institution and shall be responsible for the general status, development and the targeted use of the performing institution's financial resources. Issues relating to creative activities shall be decided by the Director on a proposal from the creative director. The stone said that, according to those who approached him, paragraph 1 was controversial and would cause confusion in their work. A manager cannot be responsible for his organization, because the development of performance institutions depends on 99% quality and repertoire, or creative. Kivi asked how the director could be responsible for the development of the theatre if he had to make decisions on creative activities on the basis of proposals from the creative leader. Anneli Ott replied that an in-depth dialogue had been conducted with the performing bodies in the drafting of the draft. The Minister confirmed that the Ministry left discussions with an agreement to amend the law. Daniel Raudsepp added that the law does not prohibit the CEO and creative director from being the same person. This is possible in practice. Whether it should be implemented, especially in the case of public foundations, is a question of its own. Signe asked if the question he asked had been a problem in the theatres. Aadu Black directed the question to Üllar Saaremäe, who replied that raising the problem is justified from the point of view of the theatre manager, that is to say, from the point of view of the financial manager, and in fact it should be dealt with. Saaremäe, in turn, asked whether the changes to the draft had really been approved by the leaders of all the theatres. Laur Kaunissaare replied that the changes have been approved by the Estonian Association of Performance Institutions as a representative organisation. According to Kaunissaar, the draft offers a solution that can be won rather than lost. Üllar Saaremäe mentioned that he was more skeptical and asked what assurance the new funding system gave the theatre leaders. Anneli Ott replied that if only ticket sales were to be used as the basis for financing, this would be counterproductive. According to the ticket sales, as we now choose a repertoire, there is a risk that performances of different tones will not reach the viewer. The Minister considered the diversity of the repertoire to be important and its reaching regional regions as well. The level of regulation provides an opportunity to set other criteria. Daniel Raudsepp added that negotiating with a representative organisation was a long process and, if you look at the state of the current law, the Ministry of Culture will also be required, as a result of litigation, to establish the methodology for evaluating theatres. Life has gone on, practice and expectations of evaluation are changes. By continuing with the current law, it will not be possible to provide certainty for the theatres so far. The problem is more in the current law, not in its new version. Eduard Odines asked which changes concern private performance agencies. Anneli Ott replied that state foundations are the responsibility of the state as owner and do not extend to private performing institutions. In the case of private performing bodies, the volume of support shall also be confirmed by the Minister on a proposal from the Committee of Experts and no political impact on the allocation of funding. The Minister confirmed that private performers will not be treated more harshly on the basis of the proposed changes. Daniel Raudsepp added that the overall picture would become clearer in connection with the proposed changes. The move to the level of the Regulation provides an opportunity to clarify the criteria, i.e. private performing bodies will see much more clearly what award criteria are and how they will receive support. It is a value in itself that provides knowledge and an opportunity to better formalise applications. Üllar Saaremäe referred to the Minister's claim that the financing system at ministerial level could also be directed towards diversifying the repertoire. Saaremäe asked for this point to be clarified. According to Anneli Ot, the explanation related to the fact that ticket sales are currently the main criteria for financing. The general playground is modest at regional level, playing performances that are full of entertainment and guarantee full halls. There is often no deeper and spiritually challenging repertoire. This is where the theaters have to make their choice. If funding criteria can be improved at the level of the regulation, the repertoire will also become more diverse. Daniel Raudsepp added that changes to the funding system give theatres a much more free choice of repertoire and, in certain cases, are not so much dependent on self-revenue. If we have a regionality criterion, it is not possible to get ticket revenue so well in certain places in Estonia. Üllar Saaremäe asked for clarification and speaking in the language of a simple person, whether if the new financing measure frees the theatres from bringing a lighter repertoire to the stage, let one Chekhov hit the other end, the Ministry will pay for the empty halls. Daniel Raudsepp replied that economic reality still needs to be taken into account, and this situation probably does not arise, so that the theatres are not expected to have their own income at all. Finally, Laur Kaunissaare said that the reality is rather this: the uniquely developed theatrical landscape, although in the case of private performing institutions, must be pushed to the limits of two criteria, which have sought to be inventive over the years, but this ingenuity has a limit which has also been established by the court. This is also the reason why the financing system needs to be changed. Then the debate on this draft ended. Aadu Black proposed that Üllar Saaremäe be appointed rapporteur for the Commission. Mr Odines presented his nomination as a Commission waitress. Signe Kivi said he would support Odinets, but he would not take part in the vote to appoint Üllar Saaremäe as rapporteur for the Commission. Mr Black proposed that the draft be put on the plenary agenda by 10 November and that the first reading be closed. It was decided to propose that the draft be put on the plenary agenda by 10 November (consensually), to propose that the first reading be closed (consensually) and to appoint Üllar Saaremäe as the representative of the lead committee. Viktoria Ladynskaja-Kubits, Aadu Must, Heidy Purga, Üllar Saaremäe and Marko Šorin were in favour and Eduard Odines was opposed. Thank you.

The same thing that several colleagues have referred to, and which has also sounded in the Commission, is how much remains behind the Minister's decision on how much remains to be decided by this advisory committee. Perhaps these tensions could be mitigated by not calling it an advisory committee of the Commission, but by calling it a funding committee, which prepares a draft decision for the minister, which the minister signs, just as very many foundations established by the state, who share state budget resources for business or other projects, do. The Minister shall verify whether the decisions have been taken on the basis of the correct assumptions, on a regular, regular and administrative basis, but shall not intervene in the decisions. However, this may happen, perhaps not with the current minister or with the next minister, but with the next minister. As Viktoria mentioned, we have heard such expressions as: how money can shut the art up. That would be one recommendation to think about, the role of this committee. As I mentioned in my question, not all grants, not all theatres or performance agencies, have this committee been created in accordance with this draft law. And here I come back to the same equal treatment that I referred to in my questions. My former manager of the small theatre is concerned by this very clear distinction between the private show agency and the foundation established by the state. I fully understand that if the state establishes a foundation, the state will be responsible for its well-being. At the same time, however, we must also recognise the people or the local authorities who, from their own resources and on a private basis, are setting up performance agencies. They also need to be reassured and supported. I see nothing wrong with a private, municipal and state-established showhouse competing with each other. So they offer better performances, better art, better outputs, not the way it was said here that Chekhov would come after Chekhov and the halls would be empty, but simply the law states that support should be given. My appeal, from which I began: we work together and try to do something in this bill that is acceptable to art critics, artmakers and politicians, who may sometimes be hurt by art. Thank you.

First reading of draft law 479 on the ratification of the Treaty amending the Treaty establishing the European Stability Mechanism initiated by the Government of the Republic. ParlaMint-EE_2021-11-24_U708	[[Remote connection]] Mr President of the Riigikogu. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! First of all, thank you for the opportunity to be before you today with a package of important drafts. The European Stability Mechanism, or the ESM known to us all, has been operational since 2012. And it has been a long enough time to assess and understand whether what was agreed following the 2008 financial crisis has worked as desired. In the big picture, the answer is yes. The crises have been resolved and new ones have been prevented. Certainly the most directly affected Cyprus, Spain and Greece as countries supported by the ESM, but the more indirect, positive impact of financial stability will certainly extend to the euro area as a whole, including us. Why is that important? This is unlikely to require any further interruption or clarification. Unfortunately, the euro area, which is in danger of stability, would also mean immediate and major problems for Estonia. The amendments to the ESM agreement that have been submitted to you today have been negotiated since 2018 and in Estonia this has been done within three governments. The last elements of the reform were tied together in the Eurogroup exactly one year ago, on 30 November 2020, when a substantive agreement was also concluded with the participation of Estonia and it was decided to submit the reform for signature. The agreement was signed in 2021, i.e. at the beginning of this year. Throughout this process, our negotiators have been guided by government positions, which have also been confirmed by the Riigikogu. The content of all the changes you are going to make today is in line with Parliament's mandate and approved positions. The three major changes concern, firstly, the establishment of a safeguard mechanism for the Banking Union; secondly, the modernisation of preventive instruments; and, thirdly, the clarification of the role of the ESM in the start-up cycle of support programmes. Firstly, the provision of a single defence mechanism function for the ESM Banking Union. The ESM will become the last resort to the Single Resolution Fund for banks. The euro area has certainly changed significantly over the last ten years. Since 2013, the Banking Union has taken a number of steps forward, a single banking supervision has been established, as well as a single resolution. The single resolution tool and, figuratively speaking, the ESM of the banking sector are a single resolution fund. In turn, the resolution fund funds are collected from banks, not through countries from taxpayers. Its target is set at 1% of covered deposits and the fund is expected to reach this level of approximately EUR 70 billion by 2024. It is a joint resolution within the Banking Union. The question has been asked from the beginning of the system, and it has been a question of how to proceed if the fund suddenly emerges in a situation where the needs are temporarily greater than the resources collected. In the fund's collection phase, the provision of this, let's say, last straw is on the shoulders of the countries. All, including Estonia, have promised to grant security loans in case of need. In our case, on the basis of the decision of the Riigikogu, it is up to 22 million euros. These instruments have not been needed. If the ESM changes enter into force, the security loan will cease to apply. If resolution is uniform, then a single last resort borrower is also needed. The logical choice fell to the ESM. Giving ESM a safeguard function is the biggest change in its content in the ESM agreement. It is a credit facility of EUR 70 billion to the Board managing the resolution fund. Loans under this limit shall be repaid by the Board within three to a maximum of five years. With the addition of safeguard mechanisms or the introduction of safeguard mechanisms, the instrument of direct recapitalisation of banks will also be removed from the ESM instruments. Perhaps, figuratively speaking, the riskier instrument under which the repayment of a loan was dependent on the ability of one bank is replaced by an instrument with a guarantee from the banks of the Banking Union as a whole. Another major part of the changes concerns the different instruments of the ESM, the conditional credit line and the additional conditional credit line. Neither option has been used for existing programmes. One of these reasons has been the uncertainty about the conditions to be met. Since then, there has been uncertainty about the arrangements for monitoring the duration of the loan, but there has also been a problem with the negative image attached to the request for assistance, the negative image attached to it. The amendments essentially standardise the conditions, also make the application process more transparent, simpler, probably less stigmatising, given that, in the case of preventative instruments, the initial assumption is that the loan will not actually be used. In the case of conditional credit lines, the Memorandum of Understanding is no longer concluded, the grant being granted on the basis of a request by the State and confirmation of intentions. In the case of a credit line with additional conditions, a memorandum shall be drawn up only in order to remedy the circumstances which preconditions were not fulfilled. The terms will be, as I said, standardised. The third set of amendments concerns the role of the ESM in the preparation of support programmes, including the assessment of debt servicing capacity during the implementation phase of the programmes, in the same way in the context of subsequent monitoring. While the European Commission has currently played a leading role in these programmes or processes, this will remain largely the case, but the ESM share will increase and the basis for cooperation, as well as the arrangements will be fixed separately in the Memorandum of Cooperation between the ESM and the Commission. Minor improvements include the requirement for euro area countries to continue to use the provisions on collective action in the bond documentation when issuing bonds, which require creditors to take decisions on all bonds at one stage. This step is important in order to facilitate the decision-making of possible debt restructuring and the implementation of these measures even before the ESM would be able to help. It will therefore provide the beneficiary with further debt servicing capabilities. The ESM amendments also create the possibility of creating a class of shares in the ESM, which would not directly require contributions to be made that would automatically decrease over time. This change has been made in view of the possible transposition of the European Financial Stability Facility (EFSF) by the ESM. And this amendment will, for the time being, simply create an opportunity to do so. Should the transposition of the EFSF become a real issue at some point, a separate decision of the Riigikogu will be necessary. Now I am going to say that all these changes to the ESM Treaty will not change the capital of the ESM, they will not impose any additional obligations on Estonia. Neither will the ESM's own decision-making procedures change. So everything that has so far been unanimous will continue to be so, for all, and for example, for the additional safeguard mechanism. The emergency procedure can still be used with specific restrictions that apply to it. The Parties' common desire and intention has been to complete ratifications by the end of this year, by the end of 2021, and to reach the launch of a safeguard measure at the beginning of 2022. However, in addition to ratifying the ESM Treaty, this requires ratification of the amendments to the SRF contribution agreement. We'll come to that later. In order for our national procedures to be able to function smoothly when dealing with ESM matters, the law on ratification and implementation of the ESM Treaty, which is also under discussion in Parliament today, also needs to be amended. The signed agreement has guided the negotiations and determined Estonia's positions. The government proposes to ratify the amendments to the ESM Treaty. I take this opportunity to thank the Committee on Monetary Affairs of the Riigikogu, which unanimously supported this proposal and unanimously gave its approval to the draft before you. Thank you. I'd be happy to answer the questions.
